**Vilnius** ([/ˈvɪlniəs/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/a/ac/LL-Q1860\_%28eng%29-Vealhurl-Vilnius.wav/LL-Q1860\_%28eng%29-Vealhurl-Vilnius.wav.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/a/ac/LL-Q1860_%28eng%29-Vealhurl-Vilnius.wav/LL-Q1860_%28eng%29-Vealhurl-Vilnius.wav.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup> [*VIL-nee-əs*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key); Lithuanian: [\[ˈvʲɪlʲnʲʊs\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Lithuanian) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/0/03/Lt-Vilnius.oga/Lt-Vilnius.oga.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/0/03/Lt-Vilnius.oga/Lt-Vilnius.oga.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>) is the capital and [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Lithuania#Cities) of [Lithuania](17675.md) and the [most-populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_the_Baltic_states_by_population) in the [Baltic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_states). The city's estimated July 2026 population was 619,405,[^cite_note-pop.lt-8] and the [Vilnius urban area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_urban_area) (which extends beyond the city limits) has an estimated population of 767,907.[^cite_note-FUA,Euro-9]

Vilnius is notable for the architecture of its [Old Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Old_Town), considered one of [Europe](9239.md)'s largest and best-preserved old towns. The city was declared a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md) in 1994.[^cite_note-archive.org-15] The architectural style known as [Vilnian Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnian_Baroque) is named after the city, which is the easternmost [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_architecture) city and the largest such city north of the [Alps](981.md).[^cite_note-unesco-16][^cite_note-VilniusBaroqueVWH-17] The [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_architecture) and Baroque architecture of Vilnius was largely influenced by the [Italian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_architecture) as Italian descent architects, artists participated in designing and decorating it.[^cite_note-Paknys2021-18][^cite_note-19] The [culture of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Italy) in Vilnius was significantly strengthened by the Italian princess [Bona Sforza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bona_Sforza), who in 1517 married the Lithuanian Grand Duke [Sigismund I the Old](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_I_the_Old), and by their son [Sigismund II Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_II_Augustus).[^cite_note-20]

The city has been the capital of Lithuania since at least 1323, when it was first mentioned as such in a [letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letters_of_Gediminas) of the Lithuanian Grand Duke [Gediminas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gediminas). Since the 13th century until the mid-17th century the [Vilnius Castle Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Castle_Complex) had served as the residence of the [Lithuanian monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Lithuanian_monarchs). Furthermore, the city was noted for its [multicultural population](#Demographics) during the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth), with contemporary sources comparing it to [Babylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylon). Before [World War II](32927.md) and [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust_in_Lithuania), Vilnius was one of Europe's most important Jewish centres, leading [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon) to dub it "the Jerusalem of the North" when he passed through in 1812.[^cite_note-21] The city's current demographics are additionally marked by [the population exchange between Poland and Soviet Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_exchange_between_Poland_and_Lithuanian_SSR) after World War II, before which Poles made up the majority of inhabitants. As of 2021, around two thirds of Vilnius' residents were [Lithuanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanians).

Vilnius was a 2009 [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture) with [Linz](55856.md) in Austria.[^cite_note-22] In 2021, the city was named one of [fDi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FDi_Intelligence)'s 25 Global Cities of the Future.[^cite_note-23] Vilnius is a financial centre, ranked 76th globally and 29th in Europe on the [Global Financial Centres Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Financial_Centres_Index).[^cite_note-24] The city is an important center for the global [fintech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fintech) industry.[^cite_note-25] It hosted the [2023 NATO Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Vilnius_summit). In 2025 Vilnius was the [European Green Capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Green_Capital).[^cite_note-26][^cite_note-27] Vilnius is a member of [Eurocities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurocities)[^cite_note-28] and the Union of Capitals of the European Union (UCEU).[^cite_note-29]

## Etymology and other names

The name of Vilnius first appeared in Latin-edited letters by [Gediminas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gediminas) from the year 1323, in the form *Vilna* (*in civitate nostra regia, dicta Vilna*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBednarczuk20103-30] In another letter from 1325, the form *Wilno* also appears (*Datum Wilno*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBednarczuk20103-30] Both forms ultimately originate from the old Lithuanian name of the tributary river [Vilnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnia) (meaning ripple),[^cite_note-31] which flows into the [Neris River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neris_River) in the center of old Vilnius, near the [Castle Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle_Hill_\(Vilnius\)). The name of the river was transferred to the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBednarczuk20103-30] The form *Wilno* is still used in the Polish language today. The Lithuanian form *Vilnius*, which is used today, was recorded at the turn of the 16th and 17th centuries in the [Postil of Mikalojus Daukša](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postil_of_Mikalojus_Dauk%C5%A1a), but it only became widespread during the Lithuanian national revival at the end of the 19th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBednarczuk20103-30]

The form *Vilna* made its way into Western European languages and, for a long time, served as the standard designation for the city of Vilnius, especially in historical and diplomatic texts. The most notable non-Lithuanian names for the city include [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *Vilna*, [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language): *Wilno* (Polish: [\[vil.nɔ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Polish) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b4/Pl-Wilno.ogg/Pl-Wilno.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b4/Pl-Wilno.ogg/Pl-Wilno.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>), [Belarusian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belarusian_language): Вiльня (*Vilnia*), [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language): *Wilna* (German: [\[ˈvɪlna\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Standard_German) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/1/1a/De-Wilna.ogg/De-Wilna.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/1/1a/De-Wilna.ogg/De-Wilna.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>), [Latvian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latvian_language): *Viļņa*, [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language): Вільно (*Vilno*), [Yiddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish_language): ווילנע (*Vilne*), [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): וילנה (*Vilna*). A Russian name dating to the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) was Вильна (*Vilna*),[^cite_note-32] although Вильнюс (*Vilnyus*) is now used.



1835 painting by [Aleksander Lesser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksander_Lesser) of Gediminas' dream about an Iron Wolf

According to a legend recorded around the 1530s, Grand Duke [Gediminas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gediminas) (c. 1275 – c. 1341) was hunting in the sacred forest near [Šventaragis' Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%A0ventaragis'_Valley), where the [Vilnia River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnia_River) flows into the [Neris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neris). The successful hunt for a [wisent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisent) lasted longer than expected, and Gediminas decided to spend the night in the valley. That night, he dreamed of a huge Iron Wolf standing on top of a hill, howling loudly. Upon awakening, the Duke asked the *[krivis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krivis)* [Lizdeika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lizdeika) to interpret the dream. The chief priest explained:

> What is destined for the ruler and the State of Lithuania, is thus: the Iron Wolf [represents a castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Castle_Complex) and a city which will be established by you on this site. This city will be the capital of the Lithuanian lands and the dwelling of [their rulers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heads_of_state_of_Lithuania), and the glory of their deeds shall echo throughout the world.

Gediminas, obeying [the gods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Lithuanian_gods_and_mythological_figures), built two castles: the Lower Castle in the valley, and the Crooked Castle on [Bald Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Crosses). He moved his court there, declared it his permanent seat and capital, and developed the surrounding area into a city he named Vilnius.[^cite_note-leg-33]

## History

Main article: [History of Vilnius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Vilnius)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Vilnius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Vilnius).

A 14th-century manuscript

The oldest known mention of Vilnius, in Gediminas' 1323 [letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letters_of_Gediminas)



[Vilnius Castle Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Castle_Complex) in 1600 with the Upper Castle (marked as 7), [Lower Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_the_Grand_Dukes_of_Lithuania) (6), and [Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Cathedral) (5)

Vilnius's history dates to the [Stone Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_Age).[^cite_note-ViVle-34] The city, at least, from 1323 until 1795 was the capital of the [Grand Duchy of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania). Later Vilnius was ruled by [imperial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) and [Soviet Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), [Napoleonic France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_France), [Imperial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire) and [Nazi Germany](21212.md), [interwar Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_Poland), and again became a capital of Lithuania in the 20th century.[^cite_note-ViVle-34]

A [Baltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balts) settlement since its foundation, Vilnius became significant in the Grand Duchy of Lithuania.[^cite_note-ViVle-34] The city was first mentioned in letters by Grand Duke [Gediminas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gediminas), who invited Jews and Germans to settle and built a wooden castle on a hill.[^cite_note-ViVle-34] Vilnius became a city when it was given city rights in 1387, after the [Christianization of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization_of_Lithuania), and was settled by craftsmen and merchants of a variety of nationalities.[^cite_note-ViVle-34] It was the capital of the Grand Duchy of Lithuania (until 1795) within the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth).[^cite_note-ViVle-34] Vilnius flourished under the Commonwealth, especially after the 1579 establishment of [Vilnius University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_University) by the Lithuanian Grand Duke [Stephen Báthory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_B%C3%A1thory).[^cite_note-ViVle-34] The city became a cultural and scientific center, attracting migrants from east and west.[^cite_note-ViVle-34] It had diverse communities, with Polish, Jewish, Orthodox, and German populations.[^cite_note-ViVle-34] The city experienced a number of invasions and occupations, including by the [Teutonic Knights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teutonic_Order), Russia and, later, Germany.[^cite_note-ViVle-34]

Under [imperial Russian rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_partition), Vilnius became the capital of [Vilna Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilna_Governorate) and had a number of cultural revivals during the 19th and early 20th centuries by Jews, Poles, Lithuanians, and Belarusians.[^cite_note-ViVle-34] [After World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Polish_Republic), the city experienced conflict between Poland and Lithuania which led to its occupation by Poland in October 1920. It remained part of Poland until its annexation by Lithuania in September 1939 and then by the Soviet Union in June 1940, becoming the capital of the [Lithuanian Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic).[^cite_note-ViVle-34]

### Independence



Leaders of the Supreme Council of Lithuania after adopting the Act of the Re-Establishment of the State of Lithuania on 11 March 1990 in the [Seimas Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seimas_Palace) in Vilnius

On 11 March 1990, the [Supreme Council of the Lithuanian SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Soviet_of_the_Lithuanian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) announced its [secession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_the_Re-Establishment_of_the_State_of_Lithuania) from the Soviet Union and intention to restore an independent Lithuania.[^cite_note-35] On 9 January 1991, the Soviet Union sent in troops; this culminated in the 13 January [attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_Events) on the State Radio and Television Building and [Vilnius TV Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_TV_Tower) which killed 14 civilians.[^cite_note-36] The Soviet Union recognised Lithuanian independence in September 1991.[^cite_note-37] According to the [Constitution of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Lithuania), "the capital of the State of Lithuania shall be the city of Vilnius, the long-standing historical capital of Lithuania".



[Konstitucijos Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstitucijos_Avenue) with most of the [high rises](118767.md) constructed in the 21st century

Vilnius has become a modern European city. Its territory has been expanded with [three acts](https://lt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lietuvos%20administraciniai-teritoriniai%20poky%C4%8Diai%201989%E2%80%932001%20m.#Vilniaus_apskritis) since 1990, incorporating urban areas, villages, hamlets, and the city of [Grigiškės](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grigi%C5%A1k%C4%97s).[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] Most historic buildings have been renovated and a business and commercial area became the [New City Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Central_Business_District), the main administrative and business district on the north side of the river Neris. The area includes modern residential and retail space, with the municipal building and the 148.3 m (487 ft) [Europa Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europa_Tower) its most prominent buildings.

Vilnius was selected as a 2009 [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture) with [Linz](55856.md), the capital of [Upper Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Austria).[^cite_note-40][^cite_note-41] The [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis) led to a drop in tourism, which prevented many projects from completion; allegations of corruption and incompetence were made;[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43] tax increases for cultural activity led to protests,[^cite_note-44] and economic conditions sparked riots.[^cite_note-45] On 28–29 November 2013, Vilnius hosted the [Eastern Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Partnership) summit at the [Palace of the Grand Dukes of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_the_Grand_Dukes_of_Lithuania). Many European presidents, prime ministers, and high-ranking officials participated.[^cite_note-46] In 2015, [Remigijus Šimašius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remigijus_%C5%A0ima%C5%A1ius) became the city's first directly elected mayor.[^cite_note-47] The [2023 NATO summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Vilnius_summit) was held in Vilnius.[^cite_note-48]

## Geography



Rivers Vilnia and Neris confluence

Vilnius is at the [confluence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confluence) of the [Vilnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnia) and [Neris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neris) rivers in southeastern Lithuania. Vilnius is 312 km (194 mi) from the [Baltic Sea](3335.md) and [Klaipėda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klaip%C4%97da), the main Lithuanian [seaport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port). It is connected by road to other major Lithuanian cities, such as Kaunas (102 km or 63 mi away), [Šiauliai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%A0iauliai) (214 km or 133 mi away) and [Panevėžys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panev%C4%97%C5%BEys) (135 km or 84 mi away).

See caption

Aerial view of Vilnius City Centre and its surrounding green space

Vilnius has an area of 402 km<sup>2</sup> (155 sq mi). Buildings cover 29.1 percent of the city; green space covers 68.8 percent, and water covers 2.1 percent.[^cite_note-49] The city has eight [nature reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_reserve): Vokės Senslėnio Slopes Geomorphological Reserve, Aukštagiris Geomorphological Reserve, Valakupių Klonio Geomorphological Reserve, Veržuva Hydrographic Reserve, Vokė Hydrographic Reserve, Cedronas Upstream Landscape Reserve, Tapeliai Landscape Reserve, and Šeškinė Slopes Geomorphological Reserve.[^cite_note-50]

Several lakes, including [Balžis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bal%C5%BEis), are located on the north-eastern outskirts of Vilnius.

One of the claimed locations for the [geographical midpoint of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographical_midpoint_of_Europe) is located in the vicinity of Vilnius, 26 kilometers from the city. The claim is based on the 1989 work by [Jean-George Affholder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-George_Affholder?action=edit\&redlink=1) of the [Institut Géographique National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institut_g%C3%A9ographique_national_\(France\)) (French National Geographic Institute) who used the [centre of gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_of_gravity) of the geometrical figure of Europe to estimate the midpoint at [54°54′N 25°19′E  /  54.900°N 25.317°E  / 54.900; 25.317  (Purnuškės (centre of gravity))](https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Vilnius\&params=54_54_N_25_19_E_type\:landmark\&title=Purnu%C5%A1k%C4%97s+%28centre+of+gravity%29), near the village of [Girija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girija_\(village\)).[^cite_note-51] A monument by sculptor Gediminas Jokūbonis, a column of white granite surmounted by a crown of stars, was built there in 2004.[^cite_note-52]

## Climate

Aerial view of the city

A foggy winter sunrise

A busy, cobbled city street

[Gediminas Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gediminas_Avenue) in autumn

Vilnius has a [humid continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate) ([Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) *Dfb*),[^cite_note-53] with temperature records since 1777.[^cite_note-54] The average annual temperature is 7.3 °C (45 °F); the average January temperature is −3.9 °C (25 °F), and the July average is 18.7 °C (66 °F). Average annual [precipitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precipitation) is 691 mm (27.20 in). Temperatures in the city have increased significantly during the last 30 years, a change which the [Lithuanian Hydrometeorological Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_Hydrometeorological_Service) attributes to human-induced [global warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming).[^cite_note-meteo-55]

Summer days are warm to hot, especially in July and August, with daytime temperatures above 30 °C (86 °F) during periodic heat waves. Outdoor bars, restaurants and cafés are frequented during the day.

Winters can be very cold, although temperatures above 0 °C (32 °F); still occasionally occur. Temperatures below −25 °C (−13 °F) are recorded every other year. Vilnius's rivers freeze in particularly cold winters, and the lakes surrounding the city are almost always frozen from December to March, and even April, in the most extreme years. The Lithuanian Hydrometeorological Service, headquartered in Vilnius, monitors the country's climate.[^cite_note-56]

## Culture

### Painting and sculpture

A large wall painting

16th-century Gothic frescoes in the Church of St. Francis and St. Bernard

Ornate brown marble sculpture

Tombstone of Lew Sapieha, c. 1633, in the Church of St. Michael

Vilnius was an artistic centre of the Grand Duchy of Lithuania, attracting artists across Europe. The oldest surviving early [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_art) artworks (14th century) are paintings dedicated to churches and [liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgy), such as frescoes in the crypts of [Vilnius Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Cathedral) and decorated [hymnbooks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hymnal). Sixteenth-century wall paintings are in the city's [Church of St. Francis and St. Bernard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_St._Francis_and_St._Bernard,_Vilnius) and the [Church of Saint Nicholas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Saint_Nicholas,_Vilnius).[^cite_note-tapybavle-63] Gothic wooden polychrome sculptures decorate church altars. Some Gothic [seals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_\(emblem\)) from the 14th and 15th centuries still exist, including those of [Kęstutis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%99stutis), [Vytautas the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vytautas_the_Great) and [Sigismund II Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_II_Augustus).[^cite_note-skulptura-64]

[Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance) sculpture appeared during the early 16th century, primarily by the [Italian](14532.md) sculptors Bernardinus Zanobi da Gianotti, Giovani Cini, and Giovanni Maria Padovano. During the Renaissance, portrait tombstones and medals were valued; examples are the marble tombs of [Albertas Goštautas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albertas_Go%C5%A1tautas) (1548) and [Paweł Holszański](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pawe%C5%82_Holsza%C5%84ski) (1555) by [Bernardino de Gianotis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernardino_de_Gianotis) in Vilnius Cathedral. Italian sculpture is characterized by its naturalistic treatment of forms and precise proportions. Local sculptors adopted the iconographic scheme of Renaissance tombs; their works, such the tomb of [Lew Sapieha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lew_Sapieha) (c. 1633) in the [Church of St. Michael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_St._Michael,_Vilnius), are stylized.[^cite_note-skulptura-64] During this period, local and [Western European](33800.md) painters created religious and mythological compositions and portraits with late Gothic and Baroque features; illustrated prayerbooks, illustrations, and miniatures have survived.[^cite_note-tapybavle-63]

During the late-16th-century [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque), wall painting had developed. Most palaces and churches were decorated in frescoes with bright colors, sophisticated angles, and drama. Secular painting – representational, imaginative, epitaph portraits, scenes of battles and politically important events in a detailed, realistic style – also spread at this time.[^cite_note-tapybavle-63] Baroque sculptures dominated [sacred architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_architecture): tombstones with sculpted portraits and decorative sculptures in wood, marble, and [stucco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stucco). Italian sculptors such as G. P. Perti, G. M. Galli, and A. S. Capone, key figures in the development of sculpture in the 17th-century grand duchy, were commissioned by [Lithuanian nobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_nobility). Their works exemplify the mature Baroque, with expressive forms and sensuality. Local sculptors emphasized Baroque decorative features, with less expression and emotion.[^cite_note-skulptura-64]

Painting of a young woman

*[Lithuanian Girl with Palm Sunday Fronds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_Girl_with_Palm_Sunday_Fronds)* by [Kanuty Rusiecki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanuty_Rusiecki)

Lithuanian painting was influenced by the [Vilnius Art School](https://lt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilniaus%20meno%20mokykla) during the late 18th and 19th centuries, which introduced [classical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classicism) and [romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) art. Painters had internships abroad, mainly in Italy. Allegorical, mythological compositions, landscapes, and portraits of representatives of various circles of society began, and historical themes prevailed. The era's best-known classical painters are [Franciszek Smaglewicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franciszek_Smaglewicz?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Jan Rustem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Rustem), [Józef Oleszkiew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Oleszkiew?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Daniel Kondratowicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Kondratowicz?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Józef Peszka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Peszka), and [Wincenty Smokowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wincenty_Smokowski). Romantic artists were Jan Rustem, [Jan Krzysztof Damel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Krzysztof_Damel), [Wincenty Dmochowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wincenty_Dmochowski) and [Kanuty Rusiecki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanuty_Rusiecki).[^cite_note-tapybavle-63] After the 1832 closure of Vilnius University, the Vilnius Art School continued to influence Lithuanian art.[^cite_note-65]

The [Lithuanian Art Society](https://lt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lietuvi%C5%B3%20dail%C4%97s%20draugija) was established in 1907 by [Petras Rimša](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petras_Rim%C5%A1a), [Antanas Žmuidzinavičius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antanas_%C5%BDmuidzinavi%C4%8Dius) and [Antanas Jaroševičius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antanas_Jaro%C5%A1evi%C4%8Dius), and the Vilnius Art Society was founded the following year.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67] Artists included Jonas Šileika, [Justinas Vienožinskis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinas_Vieno%C5%BEinskis?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Jonas Mackevičius (1872)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonas_Mackevi%C4%8Dius_\(1872\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Vytautas Kairiūkštis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vytautas_Kairi%C5%ABk%C5%A1tis), and [Vytautas Pranas Bičiūnas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vytautas_Pranas_Bi%C4%8Di%C5%ABnas), who employed Western European [symbolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbolism_\(arts\)), [realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)), [Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau) and [modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism).[^cite_note-tapybavle-63] [Socialist realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_realism) was introduced after [World War II](32927.md), with [propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda) paintings, historical and household works, [still lives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Still_life), landscapes, portraits, and sculptures.[^cite_note-tapybavle-63][^cite_note-skulptura-64] Notable late 20th and 21st century painters include Žygimantas Augustinas, Eglė Ridikaitė, Eglė Gineitytė, Patricija Jurkšaitytė, Jurga Barilaitė, and Solomonas Teitelbaumas.[^cite_note-tapybavle-63]

The [Užupis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U%C5%BEupis) district near the Old Town, a run-down district during the Soviet era, hosts bohemian artists who operate a number of art galleries and workshops.[^cite_note-68] In its main square, a statue of an angel blowing a trumpet symbolises artistic freedom.

The world's first bronze memorial to [Frank Zappa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Zappa)[^cite_note-69] was installed in the [Naujamiestis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naujamiestis,_Vilnius) district in 1995. In 2015, the Vilnius Talking Statues project was introduced. Eighteen statues around the city interact by smartphone with visitors in several languages.[^cite_note-70]

### Museums and galleries

See also: [List of museums in Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Lithuania)

Vilnius has a variety of museums.[^cite_note-GoVilniusMuseums-71] The [National Museum of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Lithuania), in the Palace of the Grand Dukes of Lithuania, Gediminas' Tower and the arsenals of the Vilnius Castle Complex, has exhibits about the history of Lithuania and Lithuanian culture.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74] The Museum of Applied Arts and Design displays Lithuanian folk and religious art, objects from the Palace of the Grand Dukes of Lithuania, and 18th- to 20th-century clothing.[^cite_note-75] Other museums are the Vilnius Museum, the House of Histories, Church Heritage Museum, [Museum of Occupations and Freedom Fights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Occupations_and_Freedom_Fights), Fight for Freedom Museum in the [Vilnius TV Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_TV_Tower), M. K. Čiurlionis House, Samuel Bak Museum, Centre for Civil Education, Toy Museum, Vilnil (Museum of Illusions), Energy and Technology Museum, House of Signatories, Tolerance Center, Railway Museum, Money Museum, Kazys Varnelis House-Museum, Liubavas Manor Watermill-Museum, Museum of Vladislovas Sirokomlė, Amber Museum-Gallery, and the Paneriai Memorial visitor information centre.[^cite_note-GoVilniusMuseums-71][^cite_note-76]

Exterior of a three-story building

The Lithuanian National Museum of Art, in the former [Chodkiewicz Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chodkiewicz_Palace)

Vilnius has a number of art galleries. Lithuania's largest art collection is housed in the [Lithuanian National Museum of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_National_Museum_of_Art).[^cite_note-77] The Vilnius Picture Gallery, in the city's Old Town, houses a collection of Lithuanian art from the 16th to the early 20th centuries.[^cite_note-78] Across the Neris, the National Art Gallery has a number of exhibitions of 20th-century Lithuanian art.[^cite_note-79] The [Contemporary Art Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_Art_Centre_\(Vilnius\)), the largest contemporary-art venue in the Baltic States, has an exhibition space of 2,400 square metres (26,000 sq ft). The centre develops international and Lithuanian exhibitions and presents a range of public programs which include lectures, seminars, performances, film and video screenings, and live music.[^cite_note-80] On 10 November 2007, the [Jonas Mekas Visual Arts Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonas_Mekas_Visual_Arts_Center) was opened by avant-garde filmmaker [Jonas Mekas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonas_Mekas); its premiere exhibition was *The Avant-Garde: From [Futurism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futurism) to [Fluxus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluxus)*.[^cite_note-81] In 2018, the [MO Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MO_Museum) opened as an initiative of Lithuanian scientists and philanthropists Danguolė and Viktoras Butkus. Its collection of 5,000 modern pieces includes major Lithuanian artworks from the 1950s to the present.[^cite_note-82]

### Literature

See also: [Lithuanian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_literature)

Old photo of a store exterior

The Zawadzki bookstore, on present-day [Pilies Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilies_Street). Its signs are in Russian, Polish, Lithuanian, French, and German.

Around 1520, [Francysk Skaryna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francysk_Skaryna) (author of the first Ruthenian [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible)) established [eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe)'s first [printing house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Printing_house) in Vilnius. Skaryna prepared and published the *Little Traveller's Book* (Ruthenian: *Малая подорожная книжка*), the first printed book of the Grand Duchy of Lithuania, in 1522. Three years later, he printed the *Acts and Epistles of the Apostles* (the *Apostle*).[^cite_note-83]

The [Vilnius Academy Press](https://lt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilniaus%20akademijos%20spaustuv%C4%97) was established in 1575 by Lithuanian nobleman [Mikołaj Krzysztof "the Orphan" Radziwiłł](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miko%C5%82aj_Krzysztof_%22the_Orphan%22_Radziwi%C5%82%C5%82) as the [Vilnius Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Academy) printing house, delegating its management to the [Jesuits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesuits). It published its first book, [Piotr Skarga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piotr_Skarga)'s *Pro Sacratissima Eucharistia contra haeresim Zwinglianam*, in May 1576. The press was funded by the Lithuanian nobility and the church.[^cite_note-84] In 1805, [Józef Zawadzki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Zawadzki_\(publisher\)) bought the press and founded the [Józef Zawadzki printing shop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Zawadzki_printing_shop). Operating continuously until 1939, it published books in a number of languages;[^cite_note-85] [Adam Mickiewicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Mickiewicz)'s first poetry book was published in 1822.[^cite_note-86]

[Mikalojus Daukša](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikalojus_Dauk%C5%A1a) translated and published a [catechism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechism) by Spanish Jesuit theologian [Jacobo Ledesma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobo_Ledesma?action=edit\&redlink=1) in 1595, the first printed Lithuanian-language book in the Grand Duchy of Lithuania. He also translated and published [Jakub Wujek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakub_Wujek)'s *[Postilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postilla) Catholica* in 1599.[^cite_note-87]

Exterior of a large church

Gate of the [Basilian Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilian_Monastery_in_Vilnius), where Adam Mickiewicz was imprisoned for fighting [Russian rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Partition)

Many writers were born in Vilnius, lived there, or are [alumni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alumni) of Vilnius University; they include [Konstantinas Sirvydas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantinas_Sirvydas), [Maciej Kazimierz Sarbiewski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maciej_Kazimierz_Sarbiewski), [Antoni Gorecki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoni_Gorecki), [Józef Ignacy Kraszewski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Ignacy_Kraszewski), [Antoni Edward Odyniec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoni_Edward_Odyniec), [Michał Józef Römer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micha%C5%82_J%C3%B3zef_R%C3%B6mer), Adam Mickiewicz, [Władysław Syrokomla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C5%82adys%C5%82aw_Syrokomla), [Józef Mackiewicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Mackiewicz), [Romain Gary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romain_Gary), [Juliusz Słowacki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juliusz_S%C5%82owacki), [Simonas Daukantas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simonas_Daukantas), [Mykolas Biržiška](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mykolas_Bir%C5%BEi%C5%A1ka), [Petras Cvirka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petras_Cvirka), [Kazys Bradūnas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazys_Brad%C5%ABnas), [Nobel laureate](21201.md) [Czesław Miłosz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Czes%C5%82aw_Mi%C5%82osz).[^cite_note-88] [Vilnius Academy of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Academy_of_Arts) alumnae have also added to the internationally acclaimed contemporary writers such as [Jurga Ivanauskaitė](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurga_Ivanauskait%C4%97), [Undinė Radzevičiūtė](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undin%C4%97_Radzevi%C4%8Di%C5%ABt%C4%97) and [Kristina Sabaliauskaitė](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristina_Sabaliauskait%C4%97). The first consideration of the [First Statute of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statutes_of_Lithuania) took place in 1522 at the [Seimas of the Grand Duchy of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seimas_of_the_Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania). The code was drafted under the guidance of [Grand Chancellor of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Chancellor_of_Lithuania) Albertas Goštautas in accordance with [customary law](218798.md), legislation, and [canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_law) and [Roman law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_law). It is Europe's first [codification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Codification_\(law\)) of [secular law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_law).[^cite_note-89] Albertas Goštautas supported the use of Lithuanian in literature and protected Lithuanian authors (including [Abraomas Kulvietis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraomas_Kulvietis) and [Michael the Lithuanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_the_Lithuanian)) who criticised the use of [Old Church Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic), and called refugees [Old Believers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Believers) as "Muscovite spies" in *[De moribus tartarorum, lituanorum et moscorum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_moribus_tartarorum,_lituanorum_et_moscorum)*.[^cite_note-Dubonis2002-90]

Exterior of a square, white building

The Institute of Lithuanian Literature and Folklore in [Vileišis Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilei%C5%A1is_Palace)

Since the 16th century, the [Lithuanian Metrica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_Metrica) has been kept at the [Lower Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Palace_of_Lithuania) and safeguarded by the State [Chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor). Due to the deterioration of the books, Grand Chancellor Lew Sapieha ordered the Metrica recopied in 1594; the recopying continued until 1607. The recopied books were inventoried, rechecked, and transferred to a separate building in Vilnius; the older books remained in the Castle of Vilnius. According to 1983 data, 665 books remain on [microfilm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microfilm) at the Lithuanian State Historical Archives in Vilnius.[^cite_note-91]

Over 200 tiles and plaques commemorating writers who lived and worked in Vilnius and foreign authors connected to Vilnius and Lithuania adorn walls on [Literatų Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literat%C5%B3_Street) (Lithuanian: *Literatų gatvė*) in the Old Town, outlining the history of Lithuanian literature.[^cite_note-92] The [Institute of Lithuanian Literature and Folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_Lithuanian_Literature_and_Folklore) and the [Lithuanian Writers' Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_Writers'_Union) are in the city.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] The [Vilnius book fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_book_fair) is held annually at [LITEXPO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LITEXPO), the Baltics' largest exhibition centre.[^cite_note-95]

### Cinema

See also: [Cinema of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Lithuania)

Exterior of the Botanical Garden gate

Billboard above the Botanical Garden main gate of the first film screening in Vilnius (1896)

The first public [film session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_session) in Vilnius was held in the Botanical Garden (now the [Bernardinai Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernardinai_Garden)) in July 1896. It was held after 1895 film sessions by [Auguste and Louis Lumière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_and_Louis_Lumi%C3%A8re) in Paris. The session in Vilnius showed the Lumière brothers' [documentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Documentary) films. The first films shown were educational, filmed outside Vilnius (in India and Africa), and introduced other cultures. [Georges Méliès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_M%C3%A9li%C3%A8s)' film, *[A Trip to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Trip_to_the_Moon)*, was first shown at the [Lukiškės Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luki%C5%A1k%C4%97s_Square) movie theater in 1902; it was the first [feature film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feature_film) shown in Vilnius.[^cite_note-cinemaLRT-96]

The first [movie theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movie_theater) in Vilnius, Iliuzija (Illusion), opened in 1905 at 60 [Didžioji Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Did%C5%BEioji_Street).[^cite_note-cinemaMIV-97] The first movie theaters, similar to theatres, had [boxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Box_\(theatre\)) with more-expensive seats. Because early films were silent, showings were accompanied by orchestral performances. Cinema screenings were sometimes combined with theatrical performances and illusion shows.[^cite_note-cinemaLRT-96]

A large, white, two-story building

The [Lithuanian Theater, Music and Cinema Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_Theater,_Music_and_Cinema_Museum), in the 17th-century [Minor Radvilos Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_Radvilos_Palace)

On 4 June 1924, the Vilnius [magistrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magistrate) established a 1,200-seat movie theater in the city hall ([Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language): *Miejski kinematograf*, City Movie Theater) to provide cultural education for students and adults. In 1926, 502,261 tickets were sold; 24,242 tickets were given to boarding children, 778 to tourists, and 8,385 to soldiers. In 1939, Lithuanian authorities renamed it Milda. The last city government gave it to the People's Commissariat of Education, which established the Lithuanian National Philharmonic Society, the following year.[^cite_note-cinemaMIV-97]

In 1965, Lithuania's most modern movie theater ([Lietuva](https://lt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lietuva%20\(kino%20teatras\))) opened in Vilnius; it had over 1.84 million visitors per year, and an annual revenue of over 1 million [roubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_rouble). After reconstruction, it had one of Europe's largest screens: 200 square metres (2,200 sq ft).[^cite_note-cinemaMIV-97] Closed in 2002, it was demolished in 2017 and replaced by MO Museum.[^cite_note-98] Kino Pavasaris is the city's largest film festival.[^cite_note-99] The Lithuanian Film Centre ([Lithuanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_language): *Lietuvos kino centras*), tasked with promoting the development and competitiveness of the Lithuanian film industry, is in Vilnius.[^cite_note-100]

### Music

See also: [Music of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Lithuania)

Title page of a libretto

[Libretto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libretto) of the first opera staged in Vilnius (1636), before the first operas in [Paris](22989.md) (1645) and [London](17867.md) (1656)

[^cite_note-101]

Musicians performed at the Palace of the Grand Dukes of Lithuania as early as the 14th century, since Grand Duke Gediminas' daughter [Aldona of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aldona_of_Lithuania) was known to be enthusiastic about music. Aldona brought court musicians and singers to [Kraków](16815.md) after marrying King [Casimir III the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_III_the_Great).[^cite_note-102] During the 16th century, composers such as [Wacław of Szamotuły](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wac%C5%82aw_of_Szamotu%C5%82y), [Jan Brant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Brant?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Heinrich Finck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heinrich_Finck), [Cyprian Bazylik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyprian_Bazylik), [Alessandro Pesenti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alessandro_Pesenti?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Luca Marenzio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luca_Marenzio), and [Michelagnolo Galilei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelagnolo_Galilei) lived in Vilnius; the city was also home to [virtuoso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virtuoso) [lutist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lute) [Bálint Bakfark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%A1lint_Bakfark). One of the first local musicians in written sources was Steponas Vilnietis (Stephanus de Vylna). The first textbook of Lithuanian music, *The Art and Practice of Music* ([Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *Ars et praxis musica*), was published in Vilnius by [Žygimantas Liauksminas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%BDygimantas_Liauksminas) in 1667.[^cite_note-103]

Italian artists produced Lithuania's first [opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opera) on 4 September 1636 at the Palace of the Grand Dukes, commissioned by Grand Duke [Władysław IV Vasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C5%82adys%C5%82aw_IV_Vasa).[^cite_note-104] Operas are produced at the [Lithuanian National Opera and Ballet Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_National_Opera_and_Ballet_Theatre) and by the [Vilnius City Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_City_Opera).[^cite_note-105]

The Lithuanian National Philharmonic Society, the country's largest and oldest state-owned concert organization, produces live concerts and tours in Lithuania and abroad.[^cite_note-106] The [Lithuanian State Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_State_Symphony_Orchestra), founded by [Gintaras Rinkevičius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gintaras_Rinkevi%C4%8Dius), performs in Vilnius.[^cite_note-107]

An outdoor stage and a crowd

The Lithuanian Song and Dance Festival in Vingis Park

[Choral music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choral_music) is popular in Lithuania, and Vilnius has three choir laureates (Brevis, Jauna Muzika, and the Chamber Choir of the Conservatoire) at the [European Grand Prix for Choral Singing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Grand_Prix_for_Choral_Singing).[^cite_note-108] The [Lithuanian Song and Dance Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_Song_and_Dance_Festival) in Vilnius has been presented every four years since 1990 for about 30,000 singers and folk dancers in [Vingis Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vingis_Park).[^cite_note-109] In 2008, the festival and its [Latvian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latvian_Song_and_Dance_Festival) and [Estonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Song_Festival) counterparts were designated as a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [Masterpiece of the Oral and Intangible Heritage of Humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masterpieces_of_the_Oral_and_Intangible_Heritage_of_Humanity).[^cite_note-110]

The [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) scene is active in Vilnius; in 1970–71, the Ganelin/Tarasov/Chekasin trio founded the Vilnius Jazz School.[^cite_note-111] The [Vilnius Jazz Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Jazz_Festival) is held annually.

A middle-aged man in a leather jacket and jeans

[Andrius Mamontovas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrius_Mamontovas), leader of [Foje](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foje) and founder of the annual *Gatvės muzikos diena* (Street Music Day)

The annual *Gatvės muzikos diena* (Street Music Day) gathers musicians on the city's streets.[^cite_note-112] Vilnius is the birthplace of singers [Mariana Korvelytė – Moravskienė](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariana_Korvelyt%C4%97_%E2%80%93_Moravskien%C4%97?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Paulina Rivoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paulina_Rivoli), [Danielius Dolskis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danielius_Dolskis), [Vytautas Kernagis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vytautas_Kernagis), [Algirdas Kaušpėdas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algirdas_Kau%C5%A1p%C4%97das), [Andrius Mamontovas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrius_Mamontovas), [Nomeda Kazlaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomeda_Kazlaus), and [Asmik Grigorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asmik_Grigorian)); composers [César Cui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%A9sar_Cui), [Felix Yaniewicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felix_Yaniewicz), [Maximilian Steinberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilian_Steinberg), [Vytautas Miškinis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vytautas_Mi%C5%A1kinis), and [Onutė Narbutaitė](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onut%C4%97_Narbutait%C4%97)); conductor [Mirga Gražinytė-Tyla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirga_Gra%C5%BEinyt%C4%97-Tyla)), and musicians [Antoni Radziwiłł](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoni_Radziwi%C5%82%C5%82), [Jascha Heifetz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jascha_Heifetz), [Clara Rockmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clara_Rockmore), and [Romas Lileikis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romas_Lileikis)).

It was the hometown of 18th-century composers [Michał Kazimierz Ogiński](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micha%C5%82_Kazimierz_Ogi%C5%84ski), [Johann David Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_David_Holland?action=edit\&redlink=1) (colleague of [C. Bach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Philipp_Emanuel_Bach)), [Maciej Radziwiłł](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maciej_Radziwi%C5%82%C5%82), and [Michał Kleofas Ogiński](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micha%C5%82_Kleofas_Ogi%C5%84ski). Nineteenth-century Vilnius was known for singer [Kristina Gerhardi Frank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristina_Gerhardi_Frank?action=edit\&redlink=1), a close friend of [Mozart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolfgang_Amadeus_Mozart) and [Haydn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Haydn) (who starred in the [premiere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premiere) of Haydn's *[Creation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Creation_\(Haydn\))*), mid-19th century guitar virtuoso [Marek Konrad Sokołowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marek_Konrad_Soko%C5%82owski?action=edit\&redlink=1) and composer [Stanisław Moniuszko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_Moniuszko). The wealthiest woman in Vilnius during the early 19th century was singer [Maria de Neri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_de_Neri?action=edit\&redlink=1). In the early 20th century, Vilnius was the hometown of [Mikalojus Konstantinas Čiurlionis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikalojus_Konstantinas_%C4%8Ciurlionis), [Mikas Petrauskas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikas_Petrauskas), and [Juozas Tallat-Kelpša](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juozas_Tallat-Kelp%C5%A1a). Late-20th- and early 21st-century musicians include [Vyacheslav Ganelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vyacheslav_Ganelin), [Petras Vyšniauskas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petras_Vy%C5%A1niauskas), [Petras Geniušas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petras_Geniu%C5%A1as), [Mūza Rubackytė](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C5%ABza_Rubackyt%C4%97), [Alanas Chošnau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alanas_Cho%C5%A1nau), and [Marijonas Mikutavičius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marijonas_Mikutavi%C4%8Dius).

The [Lithuanian Academy of Music and Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_Academy_of_Music_and_Theatre), headquartered on [Gediminas Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gediminas_Avenue), is also located at the [Slushko Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slushko_Palace) in [Antakalnis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antakalnis). Singers who have lectured at the academy include [tenors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenor) [Kipras Petrauskas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kipras_Petrauskas) and [Virgilijus Noreika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgilijus_Noreika).[^cite_note-113]

### Theatre



1687 [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) page of a theatre program dedicated to [Algirdas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algirdas), which was performed in Vilnius

The Lithuanian Grand Dukes' entertainment at the castle, rulers' visits abroad and guests' meetings had theatrical elements. During [Sigismund III Vasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_III_Vasa)'s residence in Vilnius in the early 17th century, English actors performed at the palace. Władysław IV Vasa established a professional opera theatre in the Lower Castle in 1635, where *[drammas per musica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dramma_per_musica)* were performed by the Italian Virgilio Puccitelli. The performances had basic, luxurious scenography.[^cite_note-114]

A Jesuit School Theatre existed between the 16th and 18th centuries, with its first performance (*Hercules* by S. Tucci) in 1570 in Vilnius. Baroque aesthetics prevailed at the theatre, which also had [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages) retrospectives, Renaissance elements, [Rococo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rococo) motifs, and an educational function. Performances were in Latin, but elements of the Lithuanian language were included and some of the works had Lithuanian themes ([plays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Play_\(theatre\)) dedicated to [Algirdas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algirdas), Mindaugas, Vytautas and other Lithuanian rulers).[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-theatrevle-116]

[Wojciech Bogusławski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wojciech_Bogus%C5%82awski) established Vilnius City Theatre, the city's first public theatre, in 1785. The theatre, initially in the Oskierka Palace, moved to the [Radziwiłł Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radziwi%C5%82%C5%82_Palace,_Vilnius) and [Vilnius Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Town_Hall,_Vilnius). Plays were performed in Polish until 1845, from 1845 to 1864 in Polish and Russian, and after 1864 in Russian. After the Lithuanian-language ban was lifted, plays were also performed in Lithuanian. The theatre closed in 1914.[^cite_note-117]

Exterior of a large, white building

The [Lithuanian National Drama Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_National_Drama_Theatre)

During the interwar period (when the city was part of Poland), Vilnius was known for the modern, experimental Reduta troupe and institute led by [Juliusz Osterwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juliusz_Osterwa).[^cite_note-118] The Vilnius Lithuanian Stage Amateur Company (Lithuanian: *Vilniaus lietuvių scenos mėgėjų kuopa*), established in 1930 and renamed Vilnius's Lithuanian Theatre, performed in the region. In 1945, it was merged with the [Lithuanian National Drama Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_National_Drama_Theatre).[^cite_note-theatrevle-116]

After the Soviet occupation of Lithuania in 1940, theatre became a means of disseminating Soviet ideology. Performances incorporated socialist realism, and a number of [revolutionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) plays by Russian authors were staged. A Repertory Commission was established under the Ministry of Culture to direct theatres, control repertoire, and permit (or ban) performances.[^cite_note-theatrevle-116]

Theatre changed after Lithuanian independence.[^cite_note-theatrevle-116] The independent Vilnius City Opera blends classical and contemporary art. The Lithuanian National Drama Theatre, State Small Theatre of Vilnius, State Youth Theatre and a number of private theatre companies (including OKT/Vilnius City Theatre and the Anželika Cholina Dance Theatre) present classical, modern and Lithuanian plays directed by noted Lithuanian and foreign directors. There is also a Russian-language [Old Theatre of Vilnius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Theatre_of_Vilnius).[^cite_note-119]

### Photography

Very old photo of a solar flare

A [coronal mass ejection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronal_mass_ejection), captured in 1867 with Vilnius's [photoheliograph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photoheliograph) (the second such device in the world)

According to the memoirs of architect Bolesław Podczaszyński, published in January 1853 in the *[Gazeta Warszawska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazeta_Warszawska)*, Lithuanian [photography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photography) began with the [daguerreotyping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daguerreotype) in the summer of 1839 of the reconstructed [Verkiai Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verkiai_Palace) by François Marcillac (governor of the children of Duke [Ludwig Wittgenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_zu_Sayn-Wittgenstein-Berleburg)).[^cite_note-photovle-120] The country's unfavorable political situation hampered the development of new technology and cultural activities. The first known daguerreotype-portrait [atelier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atelier) in Vilnius was opened in 1843 by C. Ziegler, and ateliers operated in Lithuania until 1859. One of the best-known photographers was K. Neupert, from [Norway](21241.md).[^cite_note-photovle-120]

In the 1860s, with the spread of the [collodion process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collodion_process), [glass negatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photographic_plate) and [albumen paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albumen_paper) were used instead of daguerreotype plates. Photo portraits in standard formats became widespread, and commercial photography ateliers were established in Vilnius and other Lithuanian cities. The first landscape and architectural photographs were made by Vilnius photographers [Abdonas Korzonas](https://lt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdonas%20Korzonas) and Albert Swieykowski, who compiled the 32-image *Vilnius Album* (Lithuania's first set of photographs). In 1862, Provisional Censorship Regulations governing the activities of photographic institutions were adopted in 1862, supervised by the Central Press Board of the Ministry of the Interior. Those who photographed the rebels in the [January Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_Uprising) were punished; A. Korzonas was deported to [Siberia](29639.md). Other prominent 19th-century photographers were [Stanisław Filibert Fleury](https://lt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanislovas%20Filibertas%20Fleris) (a [stereoscopic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereoscopy)-photography pioneer),[^cite_note-121] Aleksander Władysław Strauss, and [Józef Czechowicz](https://lt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juzefas%20%C4%8Cechavi%C4%8Dius).[^cite_note-photovle-120]

The world's second [photoheliograph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_telescope) was installed in 1865 at the [Vilnius University Astronomical Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_University_Astronomical_Observatory), and photographed [sunspots](27616.md).[^cite_note-photovle-120] An unprecedented system of photographing solar dynamics began in 1868 in Vilnius.[^cite_note-Klimka-122] [Jan Bułhak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Bu%C5%82hak) founded the country's first photography club in Vilnius in 1927.[^cite_note-123] In 1952, *Švyturys* magazine organized the city's first photography exhibition.[^cite_note-photovle-120]

### Crafts

An ornate gold monstrance

The Great [Monstrance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monstrance), commissioned in Vilnius in 1535 by [Albertas Goštautas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albertas_Go%C5%A1tautas), is one of [central Europe](5188.md)'s largest.

[^cite_note-124]

Detailed reverse of a gold coin

Reverse of [Sigismund III Vasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_III_Vasa)'s 10-[ducat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ducat) gold coin, struck in the [Vilnius Mint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Mint) in 1616, with the [coat of arms of the Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_the_Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth) and the [privy marks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_mark) of the [Grand Treasurer of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podskarbi), [Hieronim Wołłowicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hieronim_Wo%C5%82%C5%82owicz)

Iron tools, weapons, [brass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brass), [glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glass) and [silver](27119.md) jewelry have been produced in present-day Lithuania since the first century.[^cite_note-amatai-125] [Pottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pottery) wood products, and [weaving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weaving) became widespread in the second and fourth centuries. During the [feudal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudalism) era, home [crafts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crafts) were components of a [subsistence economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsistence_economy). During the 13th and 14th centuries, crafts became a branch of the economy separate from agriculture. The Grand Dukes of Lithuania promoted the development of crafts in cities, and weaving, shoemaking, fur-making and other crafts predominated. With the early-14th-century introduction of foreign artisans, the development of crafts accelerated; crafts and trade stimulated the growth of Vilnius and other Lithuanian cities. In the 14th and 15th centuries, crafts were specialized (especially the production of tools, household items, fabrics, clothing, weapons, and jewelry); [workshops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workshop) were established which trained and defended the interests of craftspeople. Production of fine [glassware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glassware) began, [goldsmithing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goldsmithing) was developed, and the level of pottery and weaving rose during the 16th century, and the 1529 and 1588 Statutes of Lithuania identify 25 crafts.[^cite_note-amatai-125] European goldsmiths worked in the [Vilnius Goldsmiths' Workshop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Goldsmiths'_Workshop) (established in 1495), which controlled trade in [precious metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precious_metal) and [gemstones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gemstone) and served the [Daugava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daugava) and [Dnieper](380138.md) regions, the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Lithuania), the Grand Duke, the [nobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Szlachta), and townspeople.[^cite_note-126] The [Vilnius Mint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Mint), the main [mint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mint_\(facility\)) of the Grand Duchy of Lithuania, minted the Lithuanian [denarius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denarius), [shillings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shilling), [groschen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groschen), [thalers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thaler), [ducats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ducat), and other coins from 1387 to 1666.[^cite_note-127]

Crafting declined in the second half of the 17th century due to the [Russo-Polish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Polish_War_\(1654%E2%80%931667\)), and most goods were imported and sold by Lithuanian and Polish nobles. It revived from the second half of the 18th century to the first half of the 19th century, with Vilnius the largest Lithuanian craft center. After the abolition of [serfdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serfdom), craft schools were established in Lithuanian cities; crafts have prevailed in [clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clothing) manufacturing, goldsmithing, woodworking, food processing, and other fields. Under Soviet occupation, craftspeople worked in [artels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artel) until 1960 and then in [combines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combine_\(enterprise\)). After independence, crafts were produced by small and medium-sized businesses.[^cite_note-amatai-125]

### Language

See also: [Demographic history of Vilnius § Historic ethnic makeup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographic_history_of_Vilnius#Historic_ethnic_makeup), and [Lithuanian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_language)

An old manuscript

Edict issued by Vytautas the Great in Vilnius on 16 February 1410 in [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin)

Title page of an old book

1783 [Lithuanian language primer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mokslas_skaitymo_ra%C5%A1to_lietuvi%C5%A1ko), published in Vilnius

An old manuscript

Manuscript of the [Constitution of 3 May 1791](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_3_May_1791) in Lithuanian

[^cite_note-Old_Lithuanian_texts-128]

A [multicultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism) city, Vilnius's language changed over the centuries. The predominant spoken language in [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval) Lithuania was Lithuanian. It had no literary tradition, however, and was not used in writing except for religious texts such as the [Lord's Prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord's_Prayer) and the [Hail Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hail_Mary).[^cite_note-kalbos-129][^cite_note-Dubonis2002-90] [Vytautas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vytautas) the Great spoke in Lithuanian with [Jogaila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W%C5%82adys%C5%82aw_II_Jagie%C5%82%C5%82o), whose son [Casimir IV Jagiellon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_IV_Jagiellon) also spoke Lithuanian.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] [Saint Casimir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Casimir), the patron saint of Lithuania, knew Lithuanian, Polish, German and Latin.[^cite_note-VilniusCathedral-133] Fifteenth-century [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) historian [Laonikos Chalkokondyles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laonikos_Chalkokondyles) reported that the Lithuanians had a distinct language.[^cite_note-134] In the 15th to 18th centuries, the most common [theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palemonids) was that the [Lithuanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanians) hailed from the [Roman people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_people) and the similarity of the Lithuanian language to [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) was emphasized.[^cite_note-Lkilme-135]

[Ruthenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruthenian_language) was used after the incorporation of [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus'), forming the basis of 19th-century Ukrainian and [Belarusian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belarusian_language). Written Ruthenian stemmed from the interaction of [Church Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Slavonic) and [Old East Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_East_Slavic) with Ruthenian dialects, becoming the main language of the [chancery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancery_\(medieval_office\)) of the Grand Duchy of Lithuania in the 14th and 15th centuries and maintained its dominance until the mid-17th century.[^cite_note-kalbos-129][^cite_note-136]

Latin and [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language) were also widely used in the chancery; Polish replaced Ruthenian in written sources and Lithuanian in public use during the second half of the 17th century. The first state documents in Lithuanian appeared in the Grand Duchy of Lithuania at the end of its existence.[^cite_note-kalbos-129]

At the Vilnius court of [Sigismund II Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_II_Augustus), the last Grand Duke of Lithuania before the [Union of Lublin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Lublin), Polish and Lithuanian were spoken.[^cite_note-137] In 1552, Sigismund ordered that orders from the Magistrate of Vilnius be announced in Lithuanian, Polish, and Ruthenian.[^cite_note-138] Minorities such as [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Litvaks), [Lipka Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lipka_Tatars), and [Crimean Karaites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Karaites) were ruled by the Grand Duke of Lithuania, and their languages were only used among themselves.[^cite_note-139] According to Article 14 of the Lithuanian constitution, Lithuanian is the [official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language); however, [interpreter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interpreter) assistance is sometimes provided.[^cite_note-140]

### Fashion

Painting of a man, standing

Painting of a smiling woman in masculine clothing

[Janusz Radziwiłł](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janusz_Radziwi%C5%82%C5%82_\(1612%E2%80%931655\)) *(left)*, wearing a *żupan* and *kontusz* belt; Emerencjanna Pociej, wife of [Ludwik Pociej](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwik_Pociej), in Western European clothing

According to historian [Antanas Čaplinskas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antanas_%C4%8Caplinskas), wives of merchants and craftsmen wore rings decorated with gemstones. Sixteenth- and seventeenth-century property inventories list long, wide-sleeved jackets (known as *[kontuszy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kontusz)*), *[żupany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%BBupan)* decorated with fur, and [kontusz belts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pas_kontuszowy).[^cite_note-clothing-141] [Buttons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Button), made of [pearl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearl), [coral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coral), [brilliant-cut diamonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brilliant_\(diamond_cut\)) and [emeralds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerald), were decorated with diamonds and enamel.[^cite_note-clothing-141] [Delias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delia_\(clothing\)) and [dolmans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolman) were popular with townspeople and nobles.[^cite_note-ragauskas-142]

Wealthy townspeople in luxurious clothing aroused the envy of Lithuanian nobility, who demanded laws regulating attire. The 1588 Statute of Lithuania limited townspeople to two rings, and Jews could not wear gold chains and [brooches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooch).[^cite_note-clothing-141] Broader restrictions were imposed by the [Sejm of the Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sejm_of_the_Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth), which adopted the 1613 Act of Thrift forbidding non-noble townspeople from wearing expensive furs in public.[^cite_note-clothing-141] Payment of a fee later removed the limitations.[^cite_note-clothing-141]

During the late 18th century, almost all men shaved; their hair was short, and they wore open-front blue, green or black [tailcoats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tailcoat) and [waistcoats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waistcoat) with white or light-yellow trousers;[^cite_note-ragauskas-142] women's clothing echoed West European styles. In the early 20th century, clothing followed West European fashion trends. The [State Art Institute of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Art_Institute_of_Lithuania) introduced clothing-design studies, and the Vilnius Model House (popularizing apparel and footwear) was established in 1961.[^cite_note-143]

The annual Vilnius spring *Mados infekcija* (Fashion Infection), Lithuania's largest [fashion show](8312977.md), began in 1999.[^cite_note-144] Lithuanian clothing designer [Juozas Statkevičius](https://lt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juozas%20Statkevi%C4%8Dius) usually presents his shows in the city.[^cite_note-145]

### Holidays and festivals

A crowded street scene

Kaziuko mugė is held each March to honor Saint Casimir

Catholic holidays such as [Christmas](6237.md), [Easter](9325.md), and [Saint John's Eve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_John's_Eve)) are widely celebrated.[^cite_note-146] On 16 February (anniversary of the Act of Independence of Lithuania) and 11 March (anniversary of the [Act of the Re-Establishment of the State of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_the_Re-Establishment_of_the_State_of_Lithuania)), festive and religious events take place in Vilnius.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148] On the evening of 12 January, bonfires are lit to commemorate the January Events.[^cite_note-149]

[Kaziuko mugė](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaziuko_mug%C4%97) (Saint Casimir's Fair), held annually in the city's markets and streets on the Sunday nearest to 4 March (the feast of [Saint Casimir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Casimir)), attracts many visitors and Lithuanian and foreign craftspeople. [Easter palms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_palm) (Lithuanian: *Verbos*) are symbolic of the fair.[^cite_note-150] Capital Days (Lithuanian: *Sostinės dienos*), Vilnius's largest festival of music and culture, is held from 30 August to 1 September.[^cite_note-151] The river Vilnia is dyed green every year for [Saint Patrick's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Patrick's_Day).[^cite_note-152] During the annual [Vilnius Culture Night](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Culture_Night), artists and cultural organisations hold events and performances throughout the city.[^cite_note-Kulturos_Naktos-153]

Since 2023, the [Vilnius Pink Soup Fest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_Pink_Soup_Fest) is organised annually to celebrate the cold beetroot soup [šaltibarščiai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%A0altibar%C5%A1%C4%8Diai).[^cite_note-luxuriousmagazine26-154]

## Administration

### Government

See also: [Voivode of Vilnius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voivode_of_Vilnius) and [Mayor of Vilnius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Vilnius)

Painting of an older, bearded man

[Krzysztof "Piorun" Radziwiłł](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krzysztof_%22Piorun%22_Radziwi%C5%82%C5%82) was [Voivode of Vilnius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voivode_of_Vilnius) from 1584 to 1603. After his victories against [Ivan the Terrible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_the_Terrible) during the [Livonian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonian_War), he was nicknamed "the Thunderbolt".

Before [Magdeburg rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magdeburg_rights) were granted to Vilnius in 1378, the city was ruled by [vicegerents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicegerent). Government was later granted to a magistrate or a city council, subordinate to the ruler. In wartime, it was led by a [voivode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voivode_of_Vilnius).[^cite_note-government-155] The government headquarters was at Vilnius Town Hall.[^cite_note-156]

The magistrate was responsible for the city's economy: collecting taxes, overseeing the treasury, and accumulating stocks of grain to avoid starvation during famine or wars. He was a [notary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notary) in transactions and testaments, a [judge](44719.md) in conflicts involving construction and renovation, and took care of craftspeople; statutes involving workshops were approved by the ruler, but Sigismund II Augustus gave this responsibility to magistrates in 1552. Since a 1522 ruling by [Sigismund I the Old](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigismund_I_the_Old), Vilnius magistrates had to protect the city and its residents with 24 armed guards. During wartime, the night watch was conducted by the magistrate, bishop and castle men.[^cite_note-government-155][^cite_note-157]

Exterior of a large, white building, with a church in the background

The [neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassicism) [Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Town_Hall,_Vilnius), designed by [Laurynas Gucevičius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurynas_Gucevi%C4%8Dius) in 1799

The chief city administrator was a Catholic *vaitas* (a vicegerent of the Grand Duke of Lithuania),[^cite_note-158] most of whom were beginning their careers in the magistracy, and chaired city-council meetings. He adjudicated [criminal cases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_case), with the right to impose capital punishment. Originally examining cases alone, two *suolininkai* also began examining important cases in the 16th century. At that time, the city council consisted of 12 [burgomasters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burgomaster) and 24 councilors; half were Catholics, the other half [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodoxy)). Members were chosen by wealthy townspeople, merchants, and workshop elders. Burgomasters were lifetime appointments; at death, another member of the council with the same religion was chosen. In 1536, Sigismund I the Old signed an edict prohibiting close relatives on the council and requiring prior agreement by the townspeople of new taxes, obligations and regulations.[^cite_note-government-155]

A large, modern building

The [Vilnius City Municipality Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_City_Municipality_Building) in [Konstitucijos Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstitucijos_Avenue), which houses the city's municipal council and administration

Under the Russian Empire, the city council was replaced with a [city duma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_duma).[^cite_note-LRistorija-159] Vilnius was the capital of the [Lithuania Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuania_Governorate) from 1797 to 1801, the [Vilna Governorate-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilna_Governorate-General) from 1794 to 1912, and the [Vilna Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilna_Governorate) from 1795 to 1915.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] After the [Soviet occupation of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_occupation_of_Lithuania), Vilnius was the capital of the [Lithuanian Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic).[^cite_note-LRistorija-159]

#### Vilnius City Municipality

The Vilnius City Municipality is the representative self-government organ, one of 60 [municipalities of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_Lithuania). In addition to Vilnius proper, it includes the town of [Grigiškės](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grigi%C5%A1k%C4%97s), as well as the villages and rural areas of the [Grigiškės eldership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grigi%C5%A1k%C4%97s_eldership?action=edit\&redlink=1).

The [Vilnius City Municipal Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_City_Municipal_Council), established in 1990,[^cite_note-LRistorija-159] is elected to four-year terms, and candidates are nominated by political parties and committees.[^cite_note-Taryba-162] Beginning with the 2011 elections, independent candidates are permitted.[^cite_note-163] Its executive organ is the [Vilnius City Municipality Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilnius_City_Municipality_Administration?action=edit\&redlink=1).

Before 2015, mayors were appointed by the council.[^cite_note-TV3merai-164] Beginning that year, mayors were elected in a [two-round system](25932.md).[^cite_note-TV3merai-164] Remigijus Šimašius was the city's first directly elected mayor.[^cite_note-165]
